- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 77 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 8,000 USD or more in 10 years. In the other 23 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 500 USD, add 50 USD every month, and want 8,000 USD in 10 years. Paid in without any growth: 6,500 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 50%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 92%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 7,120 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 9,340 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 12,300 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 2,040 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 2,040 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 12% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 21% or more in 1 in 10. About 12 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 60 USD a month instead of 50 USD
- (goal.levers[1].change) Give it 2 years more (12 years in total)
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 5 years more (15 years in total)
- (goal.levers[2].chance) more than 99%
- (goal.levers[3].change) Aim for 7,120 USD instead of 8,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 7 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 10 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 77 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 50 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 8,000 USD: 7,667 of 10,000, which is 77%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (3 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (3 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 12 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 12 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 77%. 10 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (77% chance).

- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 97 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 320,000 USD or more in 3 years. In the other 3 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 300,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 320,000 USD in 3 years. Paid in without any growth: 300,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives more than 99%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 93%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 329,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 347,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 367,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 3,040 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 3,040 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 1% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 3% or more in 1 in 10. None of the simulated futures sees a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 4 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 3 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 35 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 320,000 USD: 9,734 of 10,000, which is 97%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Investments younger than the history used (1 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 97%. 2 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (97% chance).
- (goal.shortlist[0].label) lowest risk · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[1].label) diversified · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[2].label) equal weight · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) lowest risk · bonds 100%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) equal weight · bonds 100%
- (goal.disclosures[0]) IMPORTANT: The projections or other information in this section regarding the likelihood of various investment outcomes are hypothetical in nature, do not reflect actual investment results and are not guarantees of future results.
- (goal.disclosures[1]) Results may vary with each use and over time: new market data, a change to your goal, your monthly amount, your time horizon or the funds available can all change them.
- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.
- (goal.disclosures[4]) A high chance is not a promise and a low chance is not a certainty of failure. Review the plan when your situation or the markets change.

- (goal.headline) In about 48 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 84,000 USD or more in 1 year. In the other 52 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 80,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 84,000 USD in 1 year. Paid in without any growth: 80,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 66%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 39%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 80,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 83,900 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 87,800 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 6,350 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 6,350 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 2% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 4% or more in 1 in 10. None of the simulated futures sees a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 20 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 180 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 2 years more (3 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].change) Give it 5 years more (6 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].chance) more than 99%

- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 4 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 1 year. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 11 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 84,000 USD: 4,810 of 10,000, which is 48%.
- (goal.method[5].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[5].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 48%. 4 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (48% chance).

- (goal.shortlist[1].label) lowest risk · bonds 100%
- (goal.shortlist[2].label) equal weight · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) interest tilt · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) interest tilt · bonds 97%

- (goal.disclosures[1]) Results may vary with each use and over time: new market data, a change to your goal, your monthly amount, your time horizon or the funds available can all change them.
- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.
- (goal.disclosures[4]) A high chance is not a promise and a low chance is not a certainty of failure. Review the plan when your situation or the markets change.

- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (7 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (3 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 66 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 60,000 USD or more in 8 years. In the other 34 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 25,000 USD, add 200 USD every month, and want 60,000 USD in 8 years. Paid in without any growth: 44,200 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 25%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 92%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 41,500 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 71,500 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 127,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 29,800 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 29,800 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 26% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 40% or more in 1 in 10. About 76 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 270 USD a month instead of 200 USD
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 410 USD a month instead of 200 USD

- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 36 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 8 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 69 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 200 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 60,000 USD: 6,612 of 10,000, which is 66%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (30 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (13 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 66%. 2 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (66% chance).

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 92 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 120,000 USD or more in 12 years. In the other 8 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 60,000 USD, add 300 USD every month, and want 120,000 USD in 12 years. Paid in without any growth: 103,200 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 87%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 95%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 123,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 171,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 236,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 18,000 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 18,000 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 15% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 23% or more in 1 in 10. About 19 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 42 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 12 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 83 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 300 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 120,000 USD: 9,196 of 10,000, which is 92%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (47 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (6 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 13 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 13 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 92%. 3 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (92% chance).

- (goal.headline) In about 57 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 180,000 USD or more in 10 years. In the other 43 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 100,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 180,000 USD in 10 years. Paid in without any growth: 100,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 33%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 80%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 124,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 191,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 292,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 87,800 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 87,800 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 19% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 31% or more in 1 in 10. About 45 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 190 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 420 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 2 years more (12 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].change) Give it 5 years more (15 years in total)
- (goal.levers[4].change) Aim for 152,000 USD instead of 180,000 USD
- (goal.levers[5].change) Aim for 123,000 USD instead of 180,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 8 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 10 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 77 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 180,000 USD: 5,722 of 10,000, which is 57%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (3 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (3 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 12 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 12 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 57%. 5 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (57% chance).

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In every one of the 10,000 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 160,000 USD in 15 years.
- (goal.plan) You start with 70,000 USD, add 700 USD every month, and want 160,000 USD in 15 years. Paid in without any growth: 196,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) Every one of the 10,000 simulated futures reached the goal. Each half of the history on its own: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 100%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 100%.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 291,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 351,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 423,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) Goal still reached
- (goal.severity[0].text) Even in the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would reach your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 7% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 11% or more in 1 in 10. About 1 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 5 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 15 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 89 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 700 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 160,000 USD: 10,000 of 10,000, which is 100%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (3 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (3 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 20 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 20 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 100%. 20 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (100% chance).

- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 291,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 351,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 423,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) Goal still reached
- (goal.severity[0].text) Even in the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would reach your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 7% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 11% or more in 1 in 10. About 1 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 5 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 15 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 89 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 700 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 160,000 USD: 10,000 of 10,000, which is 100%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (3 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (3 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 20 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 20 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 100%. 20 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (100% chance).

Your attitude to risk came to level 3; your category is the lower of that and the cap: C3.

- (goal.headline) In about 63 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 13,000 USD or more in 2 years. In the other 37 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 12,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 13,000 USD in 2 years. Paid in without any growth: 12,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 82%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 48%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 12,400 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 13,200 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 14,100 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 1,130 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 1,130 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 2% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 4% or more in 1 in 10. None of the simulated futures sees a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 10 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 30 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 2 years more (4 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].change) Give it 5 years more (7 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].chance) more than 99%
- (goal.levers[4].change) Aim for 12,000 USD instead of 13,000 USD
- (goal.levers[5].change) Aim for 12,000 USD instead of 13,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 4 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 2 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 24 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 13,000 USD: 6,278 of 10,000, which is 63%.
- (goal.method[5].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[5].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. This portfolio had the highest chance of reaching your goal: 63%.

- (goal.shortlist[2].label) equal weight · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) interest tilt · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) interest tilt · bonds 97%

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 60 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 200,000 USD or more in 15 years. In the other 40 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 90,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 200,000 USD in 15 years. Paid in without any growth: 90,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 28%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 88%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 104,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 234,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 504,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 137,000 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 137,000 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 34% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 53% or more in 1 in 10. About 94 in 100 futures see a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 190 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 500 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 2 years more (17 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].change) Give it 5 years more (20 years in total)
- (goal.levers[4].change) Aim for 153,000 USD instead of 200,000 USD
- (goal.levers[5].change) Aim for 103,000 USD instead of 200,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 70 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 15 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 89 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 200,000 USD: 6,027 of 10,000, which is 60%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Stocks, stock funds and crypto-assets (65 in this simulation) are picked from today's most traded list, so their past returns flatter them (survivorship bias). We kept their real ups and downs but set their average return to what their sensitivity to the world stock market earned (beta times the market's average). Investments younger than the history used (10 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 60%. 2 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (60% chance).
- (goal.shortlist[0].label) equal weight · stocks 90%
- (goal.shortlist[1].label) interest tilt · stocks 90%
- (goal.shortlist[2].label) risk-balanced · stocks 90%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) diversified · stocks 90%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) lowest risk · stocks 90%

- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.
- (goal.disclosures[4]) A high chance is not a promise and a low chance is not a certainty of failure. Review the plan when your situation or the markets change.

- (goal.label) Hypothetical simulation based on past market data; not a forecast.
- (goal.headline) In about 48 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 105,000 USD or more in 1 year. In the other 52 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 100,000 USD, add nothing more, and want 105,000 USD in 1 year. Paid in without any growth: 100,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives 66%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives 39%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 100,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 105,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 110,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].value) 7,940 USD short
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 7,940 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 2% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 4% or more in 1 in 10. None of the simulated futures sees a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.levers[0].change) Add 20 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[1].change) Add 220 USD a month instead of nothing
- (goal.levers[2].change) Give it 2 years more (3 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].change) Give it 5 years more (6 years in total)
- (goal.levers[3].chance) more than 99%
- (goal.levers[4].change) Aim for 104,000 USD instead of 105,000 USD
- (goal.levers[5].change) Aim for 102,000 USD instead of 105,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 4 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 1 year. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 11 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 105,000 USD: 4,810 of 10,000, which is 48%.
- (goal.method[5].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[5].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 48%. 4 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (48% chance).
- (goal.shortlist[0].label) lowest risk · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[1].label) lowest risk · bonds 100%
- (goal.shortlist[2].label) equal weight · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) interest tilt · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) interest tilt · bonds 97%
- (goal.disclosures[0]) IMPORTANT: The projections or other information in this section regarding the likelihood of various investment outcomes are hypothetical in nature, do not reflect actual investment results and are not guarantees of future results.
- (goal.disclosures[1]) Results may vary with each use and over time: new market data, a change to your goal, your monthly amount, your time horizon or the funds available can all change them.
- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.
- (goal.disclosures[4]) A high chance is not a promise and a low chance is not a certainty of failure. Review the plan when your situation or the markets change.

- (goal.chance) more than 99%
- (goal.headline) In about 99 of every 100 simulated futures, this portfolio reaches your goal of 110,000 USD or more in 10 years. In the other 1 it falls short.
- (goal.plan) You start with 70,000 USD, add 300 USD every month, and want 110,000 USD in 10 years. Paid in without any growth: 106,000 USD.
- (goal.precision) With 10,000 simulated futures the simulation's own error is about ±1 percentage point, so the chance is shown rounded. Which years history happens to contain matters far more: drawing only from 2007 to 2017 gives more than 99%, only from 2017 to 2026 gives more than 99%. Read the chance as a rough guide, not an exact figure.
- (goal.outcomes[0].label) 1 in 10 futures ends below
- (goal.outcomes[0].text) In the worst 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with less than 144,000 USD.
- (goal.outcomes[1].label) Half end above
- (goal.outcomes[1].text) Half of the simulated futures end above 168,000 USD and half below. This middle outcome is not a promise, and not more likely than the amounts around it.
- (goal.outcomes[2].label) 1 in 10 ends above
- (goal.outcomes[2].text) In the best 1 in 10 simulated futures you would end with more than 196,000 USD.
- (goal.severity[0].label) If it goes badly
- (goal.severity[0].text) In the worst 5 of every 100 simulated futures you would end, on average, 0 USD short of your goal.
- (goal.severity[1].label) Falls along the way
- (goal.severity[1].text) Along the way, the account falls at some point by about 4% from its previous high in a typical future, and by 6% or more in 1 in 10. None of the simulated futures sees a fall of 20% or more.
- (goal.levers_intro) Each line changes ONE thing and re-runs the same simulated futures with the same portfolio, so the difference comes only from that change.
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 5 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 10 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 77 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add 300 USD. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 110,000 USD: 9,998 of 10,000, which is more than 99%.
- (goal.method[5].title) Corrections against over-optimism
- (goal.method[5].text) Investments younger than the history used (1 in this simulation) had their missing early years rebuilt from the long-history funds they move with, adding no extra return.
- (goal.method[6].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[6].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was more than 99%. 5 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (more than 99% chance).

- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. - (goal.levers[5].change) Aim for 80,000 USD instead of 100,000 USD
- (goal.method[0].title) Market history
- (goal.method[0].text) We took the real daily price changes of the 4 investments in this portfolio from 2007-05-31 to 2026-10-16 (about 19 years, 4,877 trading days), a period that includes the 2008-09 financial crisis, the 2020 COVID crash and the 2022 fall of stocks and bonds together.
- (goal.method[1].title) 10,000 possible futures
- (goal.method[1].text) Each simulated future is built month by month: we pick a random stretch of 21 consecutive trading days (about one month) from that history, then another, and so on for 10 years. Whole months keep crashes and rebounds together, the way they really happened.
- (goal.method[2].title) Market regimes
- (goal.method[2].text) A machine-learning model (a Hidden Markov Model fitted on the daily returns and volatility of the S&P 500 fund SPY and the US bond fund AGG, 2007 to 2026) sorts every past trading day into one of four market regimes: calm, normal, stressed or crisis. Each simulated future moves from regime to regime month by month with the chances the model learned, and each month is drawn only from past months that began in the same regime, so a crisis lasts as long as crises really did instead of being broken up. Market regime today: normal (probability 98%). In about 77 of every 100 simulated futures, there is at least one crisis period lasting 3 months or more.
- (goal.method[3].title) Your plan in each future
- (goal.method[3].text) Each month the portfolio earns that month's returns (rebalanced back to its mix), pays its fund fees, and then you add nothing more. Money cannot go below zero.
- (goal.method[4].title) Counting the futures
- (goal.method[4].text) We count the futures that end at or above 100,000 USD: 2,099 of 10,000, which is 21%.
- (goal.method[5].title) The same futures for every option
- (goal.method[5].text) Every shortlisted portfolio was tested on exactly the same 10,000 futures (fixed random seed 20261017), so differences come from the portfolios, not from luck, and the same inputs always give the same result.
- (goal.choice[0]) We built 100 different portfolios, every one inside your limits.
- (goal.choice[1]) The behaviour check kept the 5 that fit how you take risk.
- (goal.choice[2]) Each of those 5 was simulated against your goal on the same futures. The best chance among them was 21%. 2 portfolios were within 1 percentage point of it, too close for the simulation to tell apart, so we chose the one of those that falls least short in the worst 5 of every 100 futures (21% chance).
- (goal.shortlist[0].label) interest tilt · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[1].label) interest tilt · bonds 97%
- (goal.shortlist[2].label) lowest risk · bonds 100%
- (goal.shortlist[3].label) equal weight · bonds 99%
- (goal.shortlist[4].label) lowest risk · bonds 99%
- (goal.disclosures[0]) IMPORTANT: The projections or other information in this section regarding the likelihood of various investment outcomes are hypothetical in nature, do not reflect actual investment results and are not guarantees of future results.
- (goal.disclosures[1]) Results may vary with each use and over time: new market data, a change to your goal, your monthly amount, your time horizon or the funds available can all change them.
- (goal.disclosures[2]) The simulation replays past market behaviour. The future can be worse than anything in the history used, and the simulation cannot show events that history does not contain.
- (goal.disclosures[3]) Assumed: the monthly amount is paid in (or taken out) every month as planned, the portfolio is rebalanced back to its mix every month, and today's fund fees apply. Not included: taxes, inflation (amounts are in future dollars), trading costs after the first purchase, and changes you make along the way.
- (goal.disclosures[4]) A high chance is not a promise and a low chance is not a certainty of failure. Review the plan when your situation or the markets change.
- (category_basis.text) Your category rests on three things: your answers on attitude to risk (level 3 of 5, two thirds), the Risk Game you played (level 1 of 5, one third) and your finances (a cap at level 5). Your attitude to risk came to level 2; your category is the lower of that and the cap: C2.
- (disclosures[0]) Past data does not predict the future. Volatility and drawdown figures are estimates from recent history and can be wrong.
- (disclosures[1]) Stress scenarios are hypothetical shocks chosen in advance. They are not forecasts, and real losses can be larger.
- (disclosures[2]) This engine makes no forecast of returns. It is designed to control risk within your limits, not to promise gains.
- (disclosures[3]) Fund fees are shown as annual percentages. Trading costs are estimates and apply only to the first purchase or to changes.
- (disclosures[4]) Risk estimates use a standard statistical method (Ledoit-Wolf) over about 252 trading days.

## Error sentences (what a person sees when the request is wrong)

- [/profile] needs more money back than invested -> HTTP 422: amount needed back by the goal date (50,000) cannot be larger than the amount invested (40,000); please check both numbers
- [/profile] invests nothing -> HTTP 422: must be above 0
- [/profile] a negative amount -> HTTP 422: must be above 0
- [/profile] a question left out -> HTTP 422: Invalid answers: GL3 is not answered
- [/profile] an answer that is not an option -> HTTP 422: Invalid answers: GL3: unknown option 'z'
- [/profile] an unknown question -> HTTP 422: Invalid answers: answers to unknown questions: ['XX']
- [/profile] an old questionnaire version -> HTTP 409: The questionnaire changed (now eq-rq-0.1.0). Please reload it and answer again.
- [/profile] game score above 100 -> HTTP 422: must be 100 or less
- [/profile] game score below 0 -> HTTP 422: must be 0 or more
- [/profile] game played in the future -> HTTP 422: the game time is in the future
- [/profile] game played long ago -> HTTP 422: the game time is before 2024
- [/profile] game with a text instead of a number -> HTTP 422: must be a whole number
- [/profile] game with an extra field -> HTTP 422: Extra inputs are not permitted
- [/preferences/form] a platform nobody knows -> HTTP 422: unsupported trading platform; supported: binance, charles_schwab, fidelity, futu_moomoo, interactive_brokers, other_us_broker, robinhood, tiger_brokers
- [/portfolio] an interest and the same industry to avoid -> HTTP 422: You chose Technology both as an industry you are interested in and as one to avoid; please remove it from one of the two
- [/portfolio] how to hold stocks without choosing stocks -> HTTP 422: The question "How would you like to hold stocks?" only applies if you chose "Stocks (shares of companies, through funds or individually)"; please remove your answer to it
- [/portfolio] an option the profile does not offer (crypto for a C4) -> HTTP 422: "Based on your risk profile, these are the kinds of investments you can make. Which would you like us to consider?": "Bitcoin and Ether" is not available to you: your risk category C4 does not include Bitcoin and Ether
- [/portfolio] an option that does not exist -> HTTP 422: One of the chosen options is not on the investment form. Please reload the questions and choose again.
- [/portfolio] a preferences version that changed -> HTTP 409: The investment questions changed (now eq-pref-0.1.0). Please answer them again.
- [/portfolio] a horizon of zero -> HTTP 422: must be above 0
- [/portfolio] a goal of zero -> HTTP 422: must be above 0
- [/portfolio] no preferences and no interest areas -> HTTP 422: send either preferences or interest_areas (exactly one)
- [/portfolio] an unreadable IBKR report -> HTTP 422: IBKR report: This is not an IBKR Flex trades report: no Trades section was found (it needs Symbol, Quantity and TradePrice columns).
- [/portfolio] an IBKR report that is an empty XML -> HTTP 422: IBKR report: The report must contain exactly one account statement.
- [/portfolio] cash buffer above 100% -> HTTP 422: must be 1 or less
- [/portfolio] a goal that cannot be met with the cash asked for -> HTTP 422: No portfolio fits all of your limits. A high cash or liquidity share is the usual cause: try a lower percentage, or choose more interest areas.
- [/portfolio] game sent twice with different values -> HTTP 422: send the Risk Game result once (inside answers)
- [/trade-check] no history sent -> HTTP 422: Send your IBKR trading history (a Flex report or a Flex token) so the check knows what you hold.
- [/trade-check] a ticker nobody lists -> HTTP 200: None
- [/trade-check] selling more than held -> HTTP 422: You hold 19,132.04 of VT; selling 900,000.00 is more than that.
- [/trade-check] a negative amount -> HTTP 422: must be above 0
